Is Sexy Art Back, or Is It Just Me?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

From Willem de Kooning’s miserable nudes to Alexandra Barth’s hot and bothered architecture, sex is in the air in Chicago.

CHICAGO — I see a lot of exhibitions in Chicago, and lately, I have been noticing more than a few that feel almost ridiculously erotic. I mean, I get it, there's always been plenty of eros in art, but I haven't observed it much lately at the school where I teach nor in the shows that I view. Is sex back? Or it could just be me. I turned 50 this past year, and popular culture suggests that this might be what happens around my age, à la Sharon Horgan's Youth and Olivia Colman's Wicker, both recent screen-based explorations of lusty midlife ladyhood.

For those who have never had one or maybe never will, female middle-age libido sets at either zero or 1,000, on a scale of one to 100. It is not an easy ride. Shifting from one state to the other causes severe whiplash for driver and passengers alike. That’s based on an admittedly minuscule survey, though fear not, this will not be a personal essay about my own sexuality or that of my acquaintances. God, no. Maybe after everyone’s dead. What proceeds, instead, is a round-up of exhibitions that, for me, have a strong connecting thread, obviously red, and not discussed much if at all in the various associated didactics.

What shows? Last spring, Alice Tippit’s sly, meticulous paintings at the DePaul Art Museum had me thinking I probably needed to start talking to a therapist again, because what was going on in my head was not articulated anywhere in the otherwise delightful catalogue. Not even close. During the summer, SHADOW at tête-à-tête-à-tête, a wicked little curatorial project of Shannon Stratton, included Holly Murkerson’s sensuous photographs of a stone passageway worth entering and two dark leather-and-wood stools by David Sprecher and Neiva Mulhearn that required straddling and peering into.

What wasn’t a turn-on was the Art Institute of Chicago’s summer blockbuster, Willem de Kooning Drawing. You wouldn’t know from the glowing reviews or polite museum text that miserable naked women filled the walls. I like a good nude as much as anyone, but at some point, maybe it was the third room — definitely by the time I got to the giant rowboat charcoals of the mid-60s — de Kooning’s subjects started to look like crime victims.

Left: Lee Miller, “Woman with Hand on Head” (also known as “Coiffure”) (1931), photograph; right: Lee Miller, “Charlie Chaplin with chandelier" (1932), photograph (both photos courtesy Art Institute of Chicago)

Arsenal still tracking long-term striker target, door still open

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Graeme Hanna is the site lead of Read Rangers and also writes about West Ham for Read West Ham. A Rangers season-ticket holder and freelance writer, he has previously written for Vital Rangers, Rangers Review, Football Ground Guide and Give Me Sport, and has a long association with the Follow Follow fanzine.

Arsenal are still monitoring Bayer Leverkusen striker Christian Kofane as they weigh up options to strengthen their attack, according to BBC Sport.

The Gunners already know the 20-year-old well.

Kofane featured for Leverkusen when the sides met in last season’s Champions League round of 16, when Mikel Arteta’s team progressed 3-1 on aggregate.

The renewed interest follows heavy speculation during the summer.

In April, Kofane’s agent Eric Depolo said Arsenal’s interest was genuine and confirmed he was in contact with the club, claiming Kofane would give them a top striker for the next decade.

Leverkusen were reported to value him at around €70m, though Depolo had talked up a €100m figure. The Gunners were said to be ‘in pole position’, but no move materialised.

Arsenal wanted a forward in the last window but missed out on Vinicius Jr and Julian Alvarez, while Morgan Rogers joined Chelsea instead.

Kofane’s rise has been rapid. He moved from AS Nylon in Cameroon to Albacete in 2024, scoring eight goals in 20 Segunda Division games, before joining Leverkusen in July 2025.

He made 44 appearances in his first season in Germany, including 11 in the Champions League, and has three goals in six games this term. The frontman is currently sidelined with a hamstring injury.

If and when, the striker leaves North Rhine-Westphalia, Arsenal face competition.

Manchester United, Real Madrid and Bayern Munich are all reported to be tracking him, amid reports his contract talks with Leverkusen have stalled.

The striker situation at the Emirates adds context. Viktor Gyokeres scored 21 goals last season but has been second choice behind Kai Havertz and has started only once in the league.

A January deal looks unlikely unless Arsenal sell one of their current forwards first.

For now, Kofane remains a long-term target rather than an imminent signing, but Arsenal’s interest has clearly not faded.

SpaceX goes for a Starship double catch

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

On Monday, Nov. 9, SpaceX is targeting 10:03 p.m. EST to launch USSF-627 on a Falcon 9 from Space Launch Complex 4 East at Vandenberg Space Force Base in California. The mission is a classified payload for the U.S. Space Force. The booster will aim for the droneship Of Course I Still Love You in the Pacific Ocean.

On Wednesday, Nov. 11, the state-owned China Aerospace Science and Technology Corporation (CASC) is targeting 6:40 a.m. EST for a Long March 2D launch from the Jiuquan Satellite Launch Center in northwestern China, according to Next Spaceflight. The payload has not been disclosed.

On Thursday, Nov. 12, ExPace, a subsidiary of the state-owned China Aerospace Science and Industry Corporation (CASIC), is targeting 2:50 p.m. EST for a Kuaizhou 11 launch from Jiuquan, according to Next Spaceflight. Kuaizhou 11 is a solid-fueled rocket designed for fast-turnaround launches of small satellites. Its payload is also undisclosed.

Later Thursday, Nov. 12, SpaceX returns to Vandenberg at 11:23 p.m. EST for USSF-317, another classified Space Force mission on a Falcon 9. The booster is again slated to land on Of Course I Still Love You.

On Friday, Nov. 13, United Launch Alliance (ULA) is targeting 12:54 p.m. EST for Vulcan Leo 1 from Space Launch Complex 41 at Cape Canaveral Space Force Station in Florida. The launch ends a nine-month grounding that followed a nozzle failure during February’s USSF-87 mission; ULA says the boosters now carry a redesigned nozzle. It’s also Vulcan’s first flight for Amazon, carrying 40 satellites for Amazon Leo, the broadband megaconstellation formerly known as Project Kuiper.

Later Friday, Nov. 13, SpaceX opens a 75-minute window at 5:45 p.m. EST for Starship Flight 15 from Pad 2 at Starbase, Texas. It’s the 15th test flight of the company’s reusable megarocket and only its second trip to orbit, after Flight 14’s success in September. About six and a half minutes after liftoff, the Super Heavy booster should return to the launch tower and be caught by its chopstick arms. SpaceX has made that catch before with the rocket’s V1 and V2 boosters, but this will be a first for the new V3 booster. The upper stage, called Ship, will continue to orbit and release 26 of SpaceX’s next-generation Starlink V3 satellites as it did in September.

You can watch the launch live on SpaceX’s website.

The next day, SpaceX will try to catch Ship, too. Until now, Ship’s test flights have ended with a practice landing at sea where the vehicle flips upright, fires its engines, and sets down on the water. This time, after about 14 orbits at roughly 171 miles (275 km) up — a flight of more than 22 hours — Ship must run that same landing maneuver over Starbase, settling into the tower’s arms instead.

SpaceX says Ship will head for the tower only if flight controllers confirm that every system it needs for the catch is prepared. If not, the company can bring Ship down at preplanned spots in the Pacific, with checks built into the flight for splashdowns near Hawaii or off the coast of Chile. If the catch works, Starship will become the first orbital rocket ever recovered in full. On Saturday, Nov. 14, Rocket Lab is targeting 12:30 a.m. EST for The Mountain God Rises, the 100th launch of its Electron rocket, from Launch Complex 1 on New Zealand’s Māhia Peninsula. Electron will deploy a QPS-SAR satellite for the Japanese company iQPS in Rocket Lab’s 11th dedicated launch for the operator. The iQPS constellation uses synthetic aperture radar (SAR), bouncing microwaves off the ground to map Earth’s surface even through rain, smoke, and total darkness.

On Monday, Nov. 2, Rocket Lab launched The Sea God Surveys from New Zealand at 11:41 a.m. EST, delivering another QPS-SAR radar satellite for iQPS. It was Electron’s 99th flight and Rocket Lab’s 10th mission for the Japanese operator. That evening at 7:15 p.m. EST, CAS Space’s Kinetica 2 lifted off from Jiuquan and placed 18 satellites for China’s SpaceSail broadband constellation in polar orbit. It was only the rocket’s second flight, following its debut in March.

On Thursday, Nov. 5, a Long March 2C launched from the Xichang Satellite Launch Center in southwestern China with three Yaogan 51 satellites. Yaogan is the label China gives its government Earth-observation spacecraft, which are widely believed to perform military reconnaissance.

Friday, Nov. 6, brought two launches about an hour apart. Galactic Energy’s Ceres 2 put four satellites in orbit from Jiuquan at 6:37 p.m. EST. Then, after several days of delays, SpaceX launched the classified USSF-432 mission from Vandenberg at 7:48 p.m. EST, landing the booster on Of Course I Still Love You.

On Saturday, Nov. 7, Russia’s Aerospace Forces launched a Soyuz-2.1b from the Plesetsk Cosmodrome in northern Russia. The payload is believed to be a GLONASS satellite for Russia’s version of GPS. The week closed Sunday, Nov. 8, when a Long March 12 lifted off from the Wenchang commercial launch site on Hainan Island at 3:24 p.m. EST, carrying nine more satellites for Guowang, China’s state-run internet megaconstellation.

On Friday, Nov. 20, CASC is targeting 6:00 a.m. EST for a Long March 8A launch from Wenchang, likely carrying another batch of Guowang satellites, according to Next Spaceflight.

On Tuesday, Nov. 24, at 2:00 a.m. EST, a Long March 12 is slated to fly another Guowang group from Wenchang, according to Next Spaceflight.

Brooks Mendenhall is a staff writer at Astronomy, based in Chattanooga, Tennessee, fueled by an unending curiosity about the universe. A former classroom teacher, he has a knack for breaking down complex concepts for a wide audience.

The Zen Mona Lisa

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A review of an ancient Chinese ink painting takes an art critic on a journey through his own past.

Every summer Jonah compiled a list of about a hundred upcoming museum shows for the newspaper’s special fall preview section. The first time he did it they were visiting Nadia’s college friend at her parents’ house in Pasadena, where he had to share a queen-size bed with Nadia and eight-month-old Orly. Fording hundreds of press releases, making decisions, and writing one-to-two-sentence synopses of complex shows he hadn’t seen while exhausted and sleepless was a trial. But the next year found him more confident, and ultimately the process became so automatic that it made no impression on him at all. If someone asked him in August, after he had filed his piece, what fall shows he was looking forward to, he would have to refer back to the document to come up with an answer.

One exhibit that did stick in his mind, though, the year Orly was six, was of a famous Chinese ink painting, a still life of persimmons, making a brief visit to the Asian Art Museum in San Francisco. It would be coming from the Japanese monastery which had acquired it when it was already nearly 300 years old and which had then kept it mostly rolled up and hidden away since the early 1500s. Jonah mentioned the show to his editor as one he’d like to cover, emphasising how unprecedented it was for the piece to travel and reminding her that he was her only freelancer with any affinity for, or knowledge of, Asian art.

She was amenable to his writing about it, she told him, but said that, because of the expense of covering the war in Ukraine, the art section had no travel budget left for the year.

“Do you really have to see it in person?” she asked.

Then cannily, or guilelessly, he added that he planned to go see it regardless. He figured that after more than a decade as an art critic, it was time to admit that art was important to him, and that so long as she did pay him to write something, he would at least break even on the experience. She offered to pay for a night in a hotel and he replied that he was planning to stay with his cousin.

“Well,” she said noncommittally, “see if you can find a cheap flight.”

Using credit card points, he found one for a couple of hundred dollars. He checked with his cousin and made plans to stay for two nights; it would be the longest he had been alone since Orly was born. Then he forgot about it, mostly. But he would mention his upcoming trip now and then to this or that person, amused to cut the figure of an adult whose job let him travel. Downtown to take notes about a conceptual art show, he met his father for coffee.

“Wow!” Alan exclaimed when Jonah told him about the ink painting. “I always wanted to see that but your mother and I were never in Kyoto on the right day.”

When the time came to go and Jonah found himself alone with a backpack on the E train, bound for Kennedy Airport, he was bemused again. As a younger man, he had travelled to escape his ordinary life, to fling himself without boundaries into unfamiliar locales. His last visit to San Francisco had been to visit a girl he’d briefly dated in New York, and he could still summon up the Pacific smell of the air when he first stepped out of the airport, the dark luxury of the apartment she was borrowing, the softness of her freckled, paper-white skin as they made love and fell asleep in a narrow guest-room bed. She had reminded him of a doe. Now, as he passed through the airport security line in New York, endured a two-hour delay caused by San Francisco air traffic related to the upcoming Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation conference, read the London Review of Books, crammed himself into an economy seat, watched half a movie on his iPad, and took a cab, at his cousin’s suggestion, to Bernal Heights, it all felt more like an errand, albeit a pleasant one, than an adventure. The air smelled of nothing. His own company felt like a childhood friend he was glad to see again, but with whom, because it had been so long, he felt shy.

First thing the following morning, Jonah accompanied his cousin, an accomplished video game designer, as he walked his dog in a nearby park. They hadn’t met in years, and Jonah felt shy with him, too — as well as rebuked in his own aimless financial irresponsibility by the cousin’s comfortably furnished 3-bedroom house with basement rental apartment attached. Looking for something to talk about, he asked whether there had been many protests about Israel’s response to the attacks of October 7.

“Not really,” his cousin said. “I did drive past one group of people with signs that said ‘Stop the Genocide.’”

His tone made clear what he thought of the protestors’ use of the word “genocide.” Not seeing any benefit in starting an argument with his host at 7:30 in the morning, Jonah changed the subject. He went and climbed a nearby hill for its view of the city; returned to the house, where his cousin fed him breakfast; and rode the bus to a still awakening Chinatown to see a show someone had told him about, work by an artist he liked at a gallery he had encountered at art fairs but never visited in person. He chatted with the young man behind the desk, asking what else there was to see in the neighbourhood and sharing his concern that the artist, a Native American ceramicist who had gotten very popular, might get boxed into grinding out the same thing over and over. Because the young man asked, Jonah also shared his name. There was a photography gallery a few blocks away, another one Jonah knew by reputation; walking there he saw a large protest of the summit that had delayed his flight.

He got past the desk and into the show — tasteful, boring photography by a successful Japanese artist, what he privately thought of as “rich people art” — with only a brief hello, but then turned around, just when he would have walked out again, to see that the gallery founder and two directors had emerged to greet him.

“They told us you were coming,” explained one of the directors, a pretty young woman in a severe business suit. Once the founder had shaken his hand and gone back to his office, Jonah let the pretty young director show him around the exhibit he’d already dismissed as well as the gallery’s back office and viewing room. He took his usual approach in such situations, making small talk as if at a cocktail party and biding his time. He admired the unique design of the display racks in the viewing room. He inquired after her family and asked where she liked to eat in the neighbourhood. As soon as he could he slipped out and hurried over to City Lights, where he bought two paperbacks by the Sicilian writer Leonardo Sciascia, whom he’d never read before.

The restaurant she recommended turned out to be an ornate Szechuan spot, deserted at that hour, whose prices he could not possibly afford. Slinking out again after looking at the menu he felt foolish to have expected anything else. Fortunately he’d overheard the clerk at City Lights mention another place, an old-school Hunanese lunch counter where there was a television playing the local news and they did not offend Jonah’s sensibilities by charging for tea. Later he rode out to the Berkeley Art Museum and Pacific Film Archive to see the exhibition of a grad school friend, another conceptual artist who made work about North American settler colonialism; the next morning, he visited a sculptor who had gone to college with Nadia and had just spent six months carving the impurities out of an enormous chunk of quartz salvaged from a commercial rock quarry.

This sculptor’s New York gallery had just closed and he had a new baby in a carrier on his chest. Jonah’s heart was in his mouth every time the sculptor leaned over his opus, bringing the baby’s head within an inch of a corner; he remembered the dirty looks he used to get from old women in Jackson Heights when they saw him carrying his own newborn infant under one arm like a football. He and the sculptor went out for dim sum and to a bookstore and the sculptor shared the advice another artist had given him about making money.

“‘Use archival material,’” he said, “‘and get used to doing the same thing over and over.’”

Then Jonah walked a mile and a half to the Asian Art Museum, which had been closed to the public to host a grand lunch for the spouses of the heads of state in town for APEC.

“Jill Biden was standing right here two hours ago,” the museum’s press chief told him, grinning and squeezing his arm, once he’d gotten past security and been led into the dim and empty gallery. “I’ll leave you alone.”

Publicists liked to talk, and curators liked to lecture, but Jonah had learned to be forthright about his need for solitude. This time they had agreed ahead of time that he would have 45 minutes to himself before the curator arrived to explain what he was supposed to think of the painting. When he was starting out, he had sometimes spent many hours over multiple days confronting a work that perplexed him. In this case, having already seen many images of what turned out to be a very easily photographed black and white art work, and having already begun to write down ideas earlier in the day, he could probably have made do with a glance. He was unlikely to experience any transcendence in such a tightly choreographed encounter, anyway. He was just too contrary. But showing up felt like a matter of principle, and after all, it couldn’t hurt.

When the press contact, a former dancer type who later surprised Jonah by mentioning his wife and two children, returned with the curator, a trim older woman in an embroidered jacket, Jonah prepared to joust. She was perfectly nice and had interesting things to say. But he needed her to understand that he was no mere journalist, to whom the Zen esthetic was news, while she, as an academic, had to demonstrate her mastery of the material. Once he felt they had both made their points, he looked at his watch, and they continued next door to a Japanese pop art exhibit. Then the curator excused herself, but the former dancer offered Jonah a tour of the rest of the museum, and Jonah couldn’t see how to refuse, even though his feet hurt and his eyes were exhausted. He hadn’t ever been there before and, as the dancer pointed out, there was no way of knowing if he’d ever be back.

When finally he left, with many thanks and professions of good will, he got on a train to a gentrified section of Oakland for dinner with a close college friend who had, after an interval in academia, become a corporate lawyer. Jonah held the friend’s new baby and then, leaving the baby and his older brother with either the night nurse or the mother in law — she wasn’t introduced — he, his friend, and his friend’s wife, also a corporate lawyer, walked to a nearby French restaurant. Jonah had been surprised, because his friend always seemed worried about money, by how nice his house was, but he supposed it made sense. The first thing his friend said, once they had been seated and had ordered some appetizers, was, “Flying across the country to see one painting is completely ridiculous.”

Jonah wasn’t sure whether he was meant to litigate this question, for entertainment’s sake, or to agree that he had wandered into an unbelievable life. He suspected the latter, but either way he didn’t feel up to it.

“Well,” he said mildly, “art is important to some people.”

“No, of course,” his friend replied, and Jonah stopped listening. In the morning he discovered that a train ran directly to the airport from quite close to his cousin’s house. There had been no reason to take a cab when he arrived. He began transcribing his notes on the airplane, and then began writing, and when this flight, too, was delayed, and they were all forced to deplane for several hours, he was delighted. The airline gave them food vouchers,  there was a decent enough sushi counter, and this way he could finish his piece and finish watching Parasite on his iPad before returning to family life. It was a beautiful experience. He was engaged in what he was doing, but with something deeper than his anxious ordinary mind; he could feel himself pirouette around the ink painting’s formal qualities and Buddhist subtext like an ice skater. “The point of this minimalism isn’t to make anything simple,” he concluded, after about 1000 words, “but to cut away distractions and reveal how complicated the world actually is.”

He filed that evening from Kennedy Airport, and when there was no reply the following day, he wondered if he should call. But then the assistant art editor sent an edit, and he dared hope he could get through the whole process without talking on the phone at all. He did end up speaking to the assistant editor, because she would need a synopsis of his piece for a meeting the following week and called to ask him to write one for her. In the course of their exchange, he mentioned that some people called “Six Persimmons” the “Zen Mona Lisa.” She suggested putting this phrase in the subheadline, and to make this intelligible, he agreed to add the fact that some people called “Six Persimmons” the “Zen Mona Lisa” to his story.

When the piece was published, he got vastly more response than he usually did, which is to say that as many as three or four people emailed him about it. One was a former college professor, who said that he wasn’t sure either of them had brought their best to the class they had shared, but that he congratulated Jonah on conserving his energies for such top-flight art writing. The next week Jonah went to Chinatown to visit an artist he might profile for a museum catalogue, a renowned older Japanese woman who had lived like a squatter in an unfurnished loft for 40 years. The gallerist who would be paying him for this, if she decided to spend what he was asking, sat between them as they chatted, interrupting their rapport from time to time to underline the parts of the artist’s life that she felt were important art historically. When she got up to use the bathroom, the artist followed her with her eyes.

“She’s okay,” the artist said. “But I think something weird about her. Like whenever we travel, she insist on getting one room with king size bed.”

On his way to Tribeca, where he was meeting his father for coffee, Jonah ran into a different art dealer, the one whose conceptual art show he had just reviewed, and she invited him into her office for a chat. They talked about marriage and their children, as they usually did, and she thanked him for the review, which he shrugged off.

She had worked at a mega gallery before opening her own space, and she told him that reviews in his paper had been the only ones the mega gallerist cared about. They appeared in sales presentations. They played a key role in what she called the mega gallerist’s “ongoing pissing contest” with his peers.

“Yeah,” Jonah laughed, “and then I get $250.”

The dealer’s jaw dropped, and he in turn stared right back at her. He couldn’t remember having ever surprised her before.

“Yeah,” he repeated. “Two-fifty for the short ones.”

“But you have so much power!” she exclaimed.

“How was the piece?” Alan asked him, once they were sitting side by side in the little cafe that now occupied the former boarding house where Frederick Douglass had first slept when he came to New York.

“To be honest,” Jonah said, “I think it comes through pretty well in reproduction.” He made a deprecating gesture. “You know, it’s black and white.”

“Oh, yeah, no,” Jonah said, “it was a great trip. I’m glad I went.”

He walked Alan back to the loft and went upstairs to say hello to his mother, to whom he couldn’t help boasting that he’d gotten some nice emails about his latest review.

“Oh, I’m so glad!” she exclaimed. “Because I saw someone on Facebook making fun of you for calling it the Zen Mona Lisa.”

Why Now Is the Time to Prepare Senior Homes for the Spring Market

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

For seniors preparing to sell a home they’ve lived in for decades, fall may be the ideal time to start downsizing before spring brings a new wave of buyers. When the weather starts to cool and the leaves start to fall from the trees, it can feel like a natural time to reset, to refresh a living space and prepare for the next life chapter.

As families shift holiday decorations, reorganize storage and prepare for the end of the year, it can also be a natural opportunity to begin sorting through a lifetime of furniture, keepsakes and belongings before a home goes on the market.

Nan Hayes, VP of Business Development at Caring Transitions, a company specializing in transforming the way seniors and their families transition through life’s stages, talks about the various aspects of preparing now for homeselling later.

Why is fall a good time to begin downsizing before a spring sale?

Fall can be a good time to adjust old habits and set new goals. As the season changes, it creates a natural opportunity to take stock of our homes and begin letting go of things we no longer need.

For homeowners considering a spring home sale, fall is a good time to begin downsizing and decluttering. Starting months before listing provides time to address repairs, sort through belongings and make decisions without the added pressure of an active home sale. As a bonus, a more organized home can also make holiday entertaining easier.

Homeowners can choose to sort, donate, sell, gift or dispose of excess belongings themselves, or bring in professionals to handle some of the more time-consuming tasks. Either way, starting early can help ensure the home is ready when it is time to list.

For real estate agents, the journey from lead to listing can take several months. Timelines become longer when homeowners are waiting for more favorable market conditions, completing home preparations or waiting on another life event, such as end of the school year, or the birth of a child.

For older adults who are downsizing into a senior living community or other care setting, the process may take even longer. Selling a family home in later life can be a major task. Many times, the home holds a lot of memories as well as a lot of ‘stuff.’ Beginning early gives families time to plan the transition and make thoughtful decisions along the way.

How can decades of belongings affect a home’s perceived space?

Even a spacious home can feel smaller when closets, cabinets, garages and rooms are filled with belongings.

Reducing excess items before listing helps buyers focus on the home rather than its contents. With fewer distractions, buyers can better understand the storage space, room sizes and possibilities the property offers.

Removing excess belongings and highly personal items can also help buyers imagine themselves living in the home. The space feels more open, and the features of the property become easier to see.

Why can waiting until a home is listed create unnecessary delays?

Once a home is listed, the clock starts ticking. If the home is not yet market-ready, the remaining tasks make the home less appealing than others on the market, and can interfere with showings, or complicate the sale and closing process.

Rushing through all the tasks at the very end isn’t a good solution either. If sorting, organizing, packing, donating, selling belongings, repairing the home, and moving all have to happen at once, the home seller can quickly become overwhelmed, and they may stop the entire process.

Starting early separates the emotional work of downsizing from the time-sensitive work of selling.

How can agents approach emotionally difficult downsizing conversations?

When clients are overwhelmed by years of possessions, unsure where to begin, or suffering from decision fatigue, they need a clear plan and calm communication that stems from a place of empathy rather than urgency.

Instead of saying: “You really need to get rid of all this!” Try: “You don’t have to figure this out all at once. Let’s start with what you would like to take with you, and then we’ll come up with a plan for everything else.”

This shifts the conversation away from what the client feels they have to give up or get rid of, so they can instead focus on what comes next. For many homeowners, belongings represent decades of memories, family milestones, and important moments. Recognizing that emotional connection can make difficult conversations easier and help clients feel supported rather than rushed.

Agents do not have to solve every part of the transition themselves. They can provide direction, connect clients with reliable relocation resources, and help break a large project into manageable steps.

How can families use the holidays to start sorting, organizing, and letting go?

The holidays may provide a natural opportunity to start on home projects because families are already spending time together. Rather than turning every family gathering into a major downsizing event, use the time to share memories, make a few decisions, and start conversations about the future.

Families might look through photographs, collections or keepsakes together. Consider digitizing large collections so they can be shared with family members. Share the stories behind meaningful belongings or write those stories down.

Invite children and grandchildren to choose items that are meaningful to them. Tackle one closet, cabinet, collection, or small area together. Identify which belongings might be donated, gifted, sold, or included in an estate sale or online auction.

Contact professional downsizing, relocation, estate sale, and auction resources early to request estimates and reserve time before spring schedules begin to fill. The goal is not to spend the holidays cleaning out the house or emptying every room. It is simply to start a conversation, make a plan, complete a few manageable tasks that can help build momentum. In short, a little progress now can mean fewer decisions and less pressure when it is time to list the home.

Michael Catarevas is a senior editor for RISMedia.

Curiosity Blog, Sols 5056-5062: Charging Back Up!

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Mars Science Laboratory Mission Team Members

By Remington Free, Operations Systems Engineer, NASA-JPL

Earth planning date: Friday, Oct. 30, 2026

Today’s plan covered 3 sols, 5060, 5061, and 5062, as we take a few recharging sols before getting back to our normal drilling activities. As you may know if you have been following the blog closely, we are in the middle of a drilling campaign on our target called “Nazca.” We’ve already drilled the hole, characterized the tailings from the drill hole with ChemCam, and have now done several sample dropoffs, both to our SAM and CheMin instruments within the belly of the rover. We still have a number of activities left to do as part of the campaign, including another dropoff to SAM for a different kind of analysis, and drilling cleanup activities. One of those activities is called “RAGE” (Rotate to Agitate for Granule Expulsion, a very fun acronym) where we essentially shake the drill to get any remaining sample out of it. We’ll also make sure to take lots of imaging to continue document the drill hole and tailings.

In any case, those activities are very power-hungry for our rover! So today’s plan purposefully did not have a lot of activities in it so we could get a nice few power positive sols before we get back to drilling. Each of our three sols had a standard morning science block, with an extra exciting brand-new activity on 5061 (more on that shortly!).

Over the three sols, we conducted remote science with ChemCam active and Remote Micro-Imager mosaics on some interesting targets (“Titicaca,” “Pampas,” and “Salta”), some Mastcam imaging of our calibration target and our drill target, several Navcam dust devil studies and Mastcam tau observations, and twilight imaging. Despite this plan being recharging/waiting, we got in lots of exciting science to continue to study the Nazca crystal formations and the nearby layered rocks and bedrock.

On 5061, we also conducted a brand-new engineering activity to test parallelism over one of our downlink communications windows. As the rover gets older and our power output slowly decreases over the length of the mission, we are continuously looking for new ways to execute activities with less power use. One of our strategies is parallelism – having two activities execute at the same time will save power due to the rover being awake for less time. However, we need to vet pairs of activities ahead of time to ensure they are safe to run together. This is a test of one of those pairs – running our CheMin “Get Data” activity at the same time as we are sending data to one of our orbiters. CheMin Get Data is a high CPU activity which transfers science data from the instrument to our flight computers. The test seems to have gone well, but we’ll have to wait to hear back from our engineering team to analyze the results!

Overall, it was an exciting plan full of great remote science as we wait to be able to wrap up our drill campaign. Stay tuned to hear more about how the rest of the Nazca campaign went!

Mars is the fourth planet from the Sun, and the seventh largest. It’s the only planet we know of inhabited…

Court Report: Tuccori Settlements on Hold as Judge Requests Further Logistics

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Editor’s note: The COURT REPORT is RISMedia’s weekly look at current and upcoming lawsuits, investigations and other legal developments around real estate.

Tuccori settlement on hold after judge request

The approval of settlement agreements with more than 20 real estate companies in a major antitrust lawsuit filed by recent homebuyers challenging commission practices in the residential real estate market has been put on hold by a judge following a request for more information.

The settlements in the Tuccori v. At World Properties commission lawsuit were preliminarily approved back in May, but final approval has been put on hold as Judge Lindsay C. Jenkins has put in a request for additional information on five topics.

Firstly, Jenkins asked for clarification on a concern that was previously raised at a hearing: The valuation of claims filed, namely that “resolving 1/8 of all claims for 1/8 of what you paid to resolve the other 7/8 of all claims does not imply you valued the 1/8 and 7/8 equally on a per claim basis.”

Other requests include how each settler’s “ability to pay and transaction volume influenced their proposed settlement,” further information for the court to be able to arrive at the “net expected value of continued litigation,” benchmarks for “substantive adequacy from other indirect purchaser class actions,” and lastly, an estimation of per claimant and per class member recovery.

The National Association of Realtors®, one of the settlers in the case, said in a statement that it “continues to believe the Tuccori settlement is fair, reasonable, and in the best interests of the class.”

“The settlement offers meaningful protections across the industry, providing a broad release for REALTOR® members, REALTOR® associations, MLSs, and those brokerages that meet the settlement’s eligibility requirements,” the statement read. “NAR remains committed to fostering fair, transparent, and pro-consumer real estate markets while providing resources and value to our REALTOR® members nationwide.”

NAR added that the judge’s request is “a routine part of the class action settlement approval process, consistent with the judge’s role in evaluating the fairness of the settlement.”

“NAR is committed to abiding by the judge’s request to provide the Court with information in a timely and efficient manner, including working with the other parties, to demonstrate the settlement is fair and reasonable,” the statement concluded.

Briefs on the topics are due by Dec. 14, after which time the court will say if it needs further responses.

Fair housing lawsuit against Bank of America dismissed

A racial-discrimination lawsuit filed against Bank of America and a Virginia-based appraiser has been dismissed.

The suit was filed by Darchelle Braxton, a Black woman who last summer attempted to secure a loan for home improvements on an investment property. She claimed in her filing that an initial appraisal ordered by Bank of America valued her property at $135,000, but a subsequent second appraisal valued it more than 50% higher, at $208,000.

The appraiser—Velox Valuations—filed a motion to dismiss, refuting Braxton’s claims that it provided a “shockingly low” valuation on her property due to alleged “material errors, omissions, and inconsistencies,” which stemmed from racial bias. Such errors included allegedly using an incorrect map reference, using an incorrect neighborhood valuation, only using two comparable properties, inconsistent descriptions of the property’s condition, and more.

The suit was dismissed with prejudice, meaning it cannot be refiled.

With the many legal battles the portal has faced as of late, Zillow CEO Jeremy Wacksman said lawsuits are “not impacting our business” during its Q3 2026 earnings call.

CFO and COO Jeremy Hofmann added that legal costs are “normalized” going forward as the company hunkers down for what is expected to be a tough environment for real estate.

“It’s not obvious to us how things are necessarily going to improve,” Hofmann said.

The portal is currently still facing a proposed class-action lawsuit from a real estate agent who claims Zillow’s “monopoly power” inflates fees and commissions. A judge ruled in September that the lawsuit could move forward.

Claudia Larsen is a senior editor for RISMedia.

Our Plea to the New DC Studios Head: No More Shared Universe!

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Four years ago, DC Studios heads James Gunn and Peter Safran relaunched the comics brand with grand ambitions to build a Marvel Cinematic Universe of their own with a crop of characters they believed to be superior to what Marvel and Disney had. They controlled Superman, Batman, Wonder Woman, Green Lantern, The Flash, and a whole bunch more they could mold as they saw fit, and they promised they would fix the flawed Marvel Studios system of greenlighting projects before a strong screenplay was in place. They announced a massive slate of 10 films and series right out of the gate and had other expectations that stars they cast today would appear in future DC films for the long haul. Above all, they were going to be careful with the brand. Related Stories DC Studios Shakeup: James Gunn, Peter Safran Stepping Down as Co-CEOs, Marvel Vet Jeremy Latcham Named Chief Creative Officer James Gunn and Peter Safran’s DC Experiment: What’s Working, What Isn’t, and What’s Next

“No one was minding the mint,” Gunn said back in January 2023 in a telling quote. “They were just giving away IP like they were party favors to any creators who smiled at them. What we are going to do is promise that everything from our first project going forward is going to be unified.”

The real world quickly caught up to Gunn and Safran. In a move beyond their control, ownership of DC Studios changed hands yet again from Warner Bros. Discovery to Skydance, and now just four years, three films, and a couple of TV shows into their tenure, they’re out. Taking the reins is Jeremy Latcham, not a household name the way Kevin Feige or some of his lieutenants have been, but an architect of the early stages of the MCU and a key, former executive at Marvel (he left for an independent label of his own in 2017). He has comic book chops, having produced “The Avengers,” the first MCU “Spider-Man,” and more.

Because the ball didn’t really get rolling at DC Studios in the short time Gunn and Safran were on board, and because Latcham still has a lingering slate that includes a second “Superman” movie that’s already in the can and a second “The Batman” movie in the works that isn’t even connected to the DCU anyway, as he begins to map out his own ambitions for the next era of DC movies and series, we have a simple plea: no more shared universes. Please. We beg you. Enough already.

Do not try and build the next MCU or even the next DC(E)U or whatever you want to call it. Just make some really good superhero movies with the characters and brands people love.

Books have been and will be written about the producing success story Kevin Feige managed with the MCU, and David Ellison, fanboy that he is, would surely love to have his own version of “The Avengers.” Who wouldn’t? We’ve seen other studios try and recreate the MCU formula with their own IP, like the Godzilla vs. Kong-iverse from WB and Legendary or the incredibly misguided plan for a Universal monster-verse that fizzled out with Tom Cruise’s “The Mummy.“

Truth be told, Marvel is trying to reconstruct its own formula ever since “Avengers: Endgame,” and it has been a mixed bag. Sure, there were a few billion-dollar hits in there like “Deadpool & Wolverine” and this year’s “Spider-Man: Brand New Day,” and “Avengers: Doomsday” too figures to be huge, but other new characters and would-be franchises like “Eternals” or “Shang-Chi” have gone through fits and stops. Movies like a “Blade” reboot have never been able to get off the ground as Marvel tries to fit a square peg into a round hole. Marvel has been forced to recast and scramble its carefully laid plans amid the controversy around Jonathan Majors. And the sheer glut of TV shows over the last few years has created immense homework for people that has led the actual flagship movies to drop off precipitously at the box office.

Is that what DC Studios really wants? More of that drama and headache? It feels like we’re well beyond the era where audiences are willing to commit for every new episode of a yearslong cinematic saga. Surely whatever vision Gunn and Safran had was grand, especially if you’re a real fan of those characters and want to see them all come together as the Justice League yet again, but it doesn’t feel like today’s young audiences want to sign up for that.

Gunn and Safran actually immediately ran into this problem as they tried to bridge the gap between the old DCEU and the new DCU they were building. People knew “The Flash” and “Aquaman 2” weren’t going to be part of the future, even as Gunn and Safran paid lip service to the idea and played coy about having some of these actors reprising their roles in the new universe. (Remember when Henry Cavill thought for a minute he was playing Superman again?) The films underwhelmed at the box office as a result, and it created confusion among casual fans as the execs were forced to dance around the fanboys and the Snyder Army.

Audiences, though, have demonstrated that superhero movies still matter when they feature characters they care about. “Spider-Man” stayed away for about five years, and “Brand New Day” is now one of the highest-grossing movies of all time. We hadn’t had a proper, standalone “Superman” movie in years, and it proved to be a great launching pad for Gunn’s slate, earning $618.7 million worldwide. There’s still huge excitement for “The Batman: Part II” from Matt Reeves, and there’s never been any pretense that it’s connected to some more important lore or that Pattinson’s Bruce Wayne might show up in a theoretical “Wonder Woman” sequel a decade down the road. People just really like Batman.

So what do you do now if you’re Latcham and have to answer to Ellison and former Mattel CEO Ynon Kreiz? It would make sense to just demonstrate how you’re going to make the most of the IP that you have. Signaling to audiences (and shareholders) that your next Wonder Woman movie won’t come out until 2037 because you’re mapping out the shared universe for how to carefully introduce all these characters doesn’t sound like the best approach. Just hire smart writers, entrust visionary directors, and make some good movies. It worked with “The Batman.”

This, of course, does not mean don’t make your big “Justice League” team up movie or don’t build a new franchise out of “Teen Titans.” It just means that times and appetites have changed from the pre-Covid and even pre-streaming boom MCU era when “Guardians of the Galaxy” could be a hit (in part) because of how it teased the arrival of Thanos and the Infinity Stones. Audiences have become more discerning about what they define as an event film, and DC Studios can beat Marvel at its own game so long as DC stops trying to become Marvel.

Starbucks Is Dropping a Surprise Box Edition of Its Fan-Favorite Bear Cups—Here’s How to Get One

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Starbucks' biggest Bearista collection yet features five festive glass characters, including one rare mystery bear named Goldie.

If there’s one thing Starbucks knows how to do during the holiday season, it’s give us another reason to celebrate. Between festive drinks in the iconic red cups and cute seasonal merchandise, there’s always something new to add to your holiday wish list. And if you’re a collector of Starbucks merch, you’ll want to mark your calendar.

The coffee chain is getting ready to unveil its biggest Bearista collection yet, and this one comes with a little surprise. In fact, think of it as a holiday collectible meets a treasure hunt.

Starbucks is making the holiday season a little more exciting with the debut of its Glass Bearista Cold Cup: Surprise Box Edition. Arriving at Starbucks coffeehouses on Nov. 10, this collection features five hand-blown glass characters, each tucked inside a surprise box.

That means you won’t know which Bearista you’ve gotten until you open it! The characters include Reiny, Joy, Aspen and Bunny, along with Goldie, a rare mystery bear that collectors will undoubtedly be hoping to find. Each character is super cute and guaranteed to bring a smile to your face as you sip your favorite Starbucks drink.

I love that Starbucks is jumping on the mystery box trend. It’s a great way to make something small like a coffee run just a little more exciting. I mean, who doesn’t love a surprise?

Ready to try your luck at finding Goldie? The Glass Bearista Cold Cup: Surprise Box Edition arrives at Starbucks coffeehouses on Nov. 10. Each box will be available for $39.95 each, while supplies last. There is a limit of two per customer, but I just know this is going to be popular. So if you’re excited, mark your calendar and be at your local Starbucks early.

Curiosity Blog, Sols 5050-5055: It’s Nazca, Our 49th Drill Hole

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Mars Science Laboratory Mission Team Members

Written by Susanne P. Schwenzer, Professor of Planetary Mineralogy at The Open University, UK

Earth planning date: Friday, Oct. 23, 2026

As regular readers of this blog will know, we returned to the area where we found disc shaped inclusions in the rocks, which we interpreted as potential crystals. If they are indeed crystals, and we compare them to Earth, they could be very interesting indicators of the environmental conditions. Hence, the excitement in the team is high that we found a good block to drill. Good means first and foremost that it contains abundant potential crystals, but of course there is more before we drill. We passed the rover’s stability test, as all wheels have to be on solid ground before we extend the arm. Then we passed the preload test, which tests the stability of the rock and the forces the arm experiences when the drill bit presses into the rock.

With all those tests passed, we successfully drilled our 49th drill hole, called “Nazca.”

The drill campaign comes with many investigations before and after the drilling itself. Before the drill, we investigated the block with APXS, MAHLI and ChemCam – all on the target Nazca. After drilling, the sample was dropped off to CheMin, with the results still making their way from Mars to Earth. They are eagerly anticipated to reach us over the coming weekend. ChemCam obtained both passive spectral and active LIBS of the tailings and a LIBS measurement down the drill hole wall.

Other ChemCam activities this week included a LIBS investigation of “Palpala,” which is a dark pebble. We are on the lookout for those pebbles for a while and find that not all of them have the same composition. To make sure we understand their origin, we are measuring them when there are some in reach that are big enough. If a pebble is too small, it can move when it is being hit by the laser. Hence, we need to look out for some that are big enough.

Another such pebble was the target “Puna” identified in Friday’s planning. ChemCam LIBS on the target “Llama” is to get another depth profile to check whether our observations in the first such depth profile are consistently observed or whether they vary between locations.

The target “Pacaya” is another measurement of the disc shaped forms that drew us back to this region. In addition to the LIBS measurements, ChemCam has several long distance RMIs in the plan. They are all focused on the two buttes that have drawn a long-standing interest of the team for the various sedimentary structures and textures they reveal: Mishe Mokwa and Cordillera.

Psychedelics Linked to Lower Anxiety and Depression in Young Adults

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

An epidemiological investigation from Durham University reveals that the mental health benefits linked to classic psychedelics, such as psilocybin and LSD, vary significantly across human lifespans. Published in Scientific Reports, the study analyzed survey data from 1,088 adults aged 18 to 55 and older, evaluating lifetime psychedelic use alongside validated anxiety, depression, and well-being scales.

Researchers discovered that adults in their twenties and early thirties with a history of classic psychedelic use reported significantly lower levels of depression and anxiety compared to non-users. However, this mental health association diminished progressively with age and disappeared entirely in older cohorts, a divergence largely explained by the higher intensity of mystical-type experiences reported by younger individuals.

Source: Durham University / Scientific Reports

As psychedelic-assisted psychotherapy moves through advanced clinical trials for major depressive disorder, generalized anxiety, and treatment-resistant post-traumatic stress, neuroscientists are working to understand the demographic, psychological, and neurobiological parameters that dictate therapeutic efficacy.

While clinical trials focus on short-term symptom reduction under structured medical supervision, evaluating the long-term mental health footprints of naturalistic psychedelic use across diverse populations remains essential for establishing realistic clinical expectations.

In an investigation published in Scientific Reports (a Nature Portfolio journal), researchers at Durham University in the United Kingdom evaluated the relationship between lifetime psychedelic use and contemporary mental health across the adult lifespan.

Their findings demonstrate that age plays a governing role: while young adults who have used classic psychedelics show marked reductions in anxiety and depression, this protective association fades systematically with advancing age.

Led by doctoral researcher Giammarco Di Gregorio under the supervision of neurobiologist Dr. Marco Bocchio and psychologist Dr. Michael Lengieza at Durham University’s Department of Psychology, the team analyzed responses from 1,088 adults aged 18 to over 55.

Participants provided comprehensive histories of their lifetime psychoactive substance use and completed validated psychometric batteries measuring depression, anxiety, subjective well-being, and life satisfaction.

To isolate true substance-specific outcomes from general sensation-seeking behaviors, the researchers partitioned respondents into four distinct analytical cohorts:

This inclusion of non-classic users served as a rigorous methodological control, allowing the team to test whether psychological differences were driven by classic psychedelics specifically or merely by general personality traits common among people drawn to altered states of consciousness.

Cross-sectional analysis revealed a clear demographic divergence. Among adults in their twenties and early thirties, a history of classic psychedelic use was robustly tied to lower baseline anxiety and depression scores relative to non-user peers.

However, as participant age increased, this positive association steadily attenuated. In cohorts aged 40 and older—and particularly those 55 and above—the mental health difference between classic psychedelic users and non-users flattened entirely.

The association held true even after controlling for potential confounding factors, including lifetime frequency of use, elapsed time since last exposure, and typical dosage.

The researchers traced the root of this age divergence to the phenomenological quality of the psychedelic state itself.

“Age remains a relatively underexplored factor in psychedelic research,” said lead author Giammarco Di Gregorio. “There’s growing evidence that younger people tend to have more intense, mystical-type experiences while on psychedelics. Our study suggests this may matter for mental health. The link between classic psychedelic use and lower anxiety and depression was strongest in younger adults and faded with age, and the mystical experiences younger users reported helped to explain that pattern.”

Because psychedelic clinical trials typically enroll tightly filtered, relatively narrow demographic bands, large-scale naturalistic studies provide essential clues for clinical trial design.

“One of the strengths of this study is that it allowed us to examine psychedelic use and mental health associations in a large sample with a broad age range, which can be difficult to achieve in clinical trials,” said co-supervisor Dr. Marco Bocchio. “By analysing data from more than 1,000 participants, we were able to identify age-related patterns that might otherwise be missed.”

Bocchio highlighted the importance of their pharmacological control group: “A novelty of our study was the inclusion of a comparison group of people who had used ‘non classic psychedelics’, such as ketamine or MDMA, but not classic psychedelics. This helped us test whether the patterns we observed were specific to classic psychedelics, rather than reflecting broader differences between people who are drawn to psychoactive substances and altered states of consciousness.”

The investigators emphasize that the cross-sectional, self-reported design of the study demonstrates an epidemiological association, not direct biological causation. It remains plausible that younger individuals with greater baseline psychological resilience or specific neuroplastic profiles are both more likely to navigate intense mystical experiences and less vulnerable to mood disorders.

To clarify the causal mechanisms, the Durham team is preparing longitudinal protocols that track cognitive metrics, personality shifts, and mental health trajectories before and after psychedelic administration across different decades of life.

As regulatory agencies evaluate psychedelic therapies, factoring in biological aging, receptor density changes, and subjective experience intensity will be vital for determining which patients stand to benefit most from these emergent treatments.

What was the main finding of the Durham University psychedelic study?

The study found that adults in their twenties and early thirties who had used classic psychedelics (like psilocybin and LSD) reported lower levels of anxiety and depression than non-users. However, this positive mental health link weakened with age and was completely absent in older adults.

Why does the relationship between psychedelics and mental health fade with age?

The researchers found that younger adults report significantly more intense “mystical-type” experiences during their trips. These subjective mystical experiences mediated the link to lower anxiety and depression, suggesting that younger brains may be more receptive to the transformative psychological effects of psychedelics.

Does this study prove that taking psilocybin or LSD cures depression in young adults?

No. The study is observational and cross-sectional, relying on self-reported retrospective surveys. While it identifies an intriguing age-stratified association, it cannot prove direct cause and effect, nor does it suggest individuals should self-medicate outside of clinical settings.

Age moderates the relationship between psychedelic use and mental health in naturalistic settings

Depression and anxiety affect one in five adults and vary across the lifespan; while prevalence generally declines with age until midlife, later life is marked by a re-emergence of symptoms. Although clinical trials suggest classic psychedelics (e.g., psilocybin, LSD) and non-classic psychedelics (e.g., MDMA, ketamine) may alleviate these symptoms, it remains unclear how these relationships function in naturalistic settings or how they vary across the lifespan.

We conducted a cross-sectional survey of 1,088 adults (18–55 + years) to assess how lifetime psychedelic use – categorised as classic, non-classic, or mixed – relates to mental health. We found that age significantly moderates the relationship between lifetime history of psychedelic use and mental health measures. Specifically, classic psychedelic use was linked to lower depression and anxiety among younger adults, but these effects diminished with age.

These associations persisted independently of drug-use parameters, including dosage, frequency, and recency of use. Moderated-mediation analyses indicated that mystical-type experiences accounted for the associations with both depression and anxiety among younger adults, but not among older adults, where a residual association with anxiety remained independent of mystical experience.

These findings suggest that age may be a meaningful moderator of mental health outcomes from psychedelic use. This underscores the potential value of age-stratified research to optimise the efficacy and safety of psychedelic-assisted interventions.

Significant Pent-Up Demand Could Be Released If Supply, Affordability and Confidence Improve

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A wave of sidelined homebuyers could be poised to flood the housing market if a trifecta of housing supply and affordability improve and consumer confidence picks up. How likely that is remains unclear, however.

That was the theme of a market outlook delivered this past Friday by National Association of Realtors® Chief Economist Lawrence Yun at the Residential Economic Issues and Trends Forum at NAR NXT in New Orleans.

According to a release, Yun compared today’s economy with the year 2000, noting since then, the U.S. population has grown from 281 million to 350 million, and payroll jobs have risen from 132 million to 159 million. He said housing wealth has grown from $7 trillion to $35 trillion, yet existing-home sales are running near 4 million this year, below the 5.2 million homes sold in 2000.

“Affordability is tough, but people’s incomes are rising, and if somehow mortgage rates go down, think about the potential housing demand that could be released to the marketplace,” Yun said.

On supply, Yun noted that inventory has risen as sales have slowed. Months’ supply is at its highest level since 2015, and the median home took 33 days to go from listing to contract in September.

“Homes are staying on the market a little longer than before,” Yun said. “With longer days on market, you have to be realistic to the market condition. Otherwise, buyers will reject the homes on the market.”

Yun said a rebound in consumer confidence would also be key. Consumer sentiment has fallen to roughly half its 2000 level and is now worse than during the Great Recession, even as the economy has added jobs, he said.

“If we can boost confidence levels, maybe that can also change the dynamics,” Yun said.

Jessica Lautz, NAR deputy chief economist and vice president of research, presented highlights from the newly released 2026 Profile of Home Buyers and Sellers. First-time buyers made up 22% of all buyers, near the historic low, and the median age of first-time buyers held at a record-high 40.

“First-time home buyers are struggling,” Lautz said. “They’re not struggling just with housing affordability. They’re also struggling with rent. They’re struggling with student loan debt, and a rising issue for first-time home buyers is childcare costs.”

Sellers stayed in their homes for a median of 12 years before selling, the longest on record.

“The golden handcuffs of low interest rate mortgages is true. People are holding on to those properties for longer periods of time,” said Lautz. “It’s why we need to build more housing inventory—especially affordable housing inventory—for the potential first-time home buyers who are out there.”

Use of real estate professionals remained strong: 90% of buyers purchased through an agent, the highest share in four decades, and a record 92% of sellers used an agent.

“Sellers want agents to price that home competitively, find that qualified buyer, help with marketing, and make sure they get to the closing table in a time frame that they need to do so,” Lautz said.

Routine maintenance, sensible storage, suitable flood protection and a clear emergency plan can help homeowners prepare more effectively. The right approach depends on the property, its surroundings and the conditions it may face. Read more.

Paid Search Is Moving Up The Funnel – Google Admits AI Mode Pays Less via @sejournal, @gregjarboe

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Calls from ChatGPT and Claude often get filed as direct. This checklist shows how to track AI search as its own call source.

Calls from ChatGPT and Claude often get filed as direct. This checklist shows how to track AI search as its own call source.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Guide: AI Max Drove 68% Of Retail Invalid Clicks

Protect PPC budgets as Google's AI Mode shifts queries up-funnel, with four account changes to test new inventory and prove whether discovery pays.

Google’s head of search data science told advertisers on Oct. 28 that people now come to Google earlier in their buying journeys, asking long, detailed questions that start a long way from a purchase. That same afternoon, Alphabet’s chief business officer told Wall Street that the AI Mode queries driving that shift make Google less money than traditional searches do.

Put those two statements side by side, and you have the planning problem facing every paid search manager heading into 2027. The queries are arriving at the top of the funnel, while most Google Ads accounts are still judged at the bottom.

This column pulls the advertiser lessons out of that conversation, checks its numbers against what Google has published elsewhere, and turns it into four changes you can make in your accounts before Black Friday.

On Google’s Ads Decoded podcast, Oct. 28, host Ginny Marvin opens by saying the show is “getting out of the advertising world and into the organic search world” for the day.

Her guest is Shivani Mohan, Google Vice President of Data Science and User Experience Research (UXR) for Search. Mohan’s UXR teams interview people one-on-one to learn why they phrase questions the way they do, and her data scientists study logs of what billions of users actually do. She started her career designing bicycles, where she says, “you get one shot to really nail the user understanding” (3:04) because a finished product can’t be iterated.

Google can iterate on Search every day. Advertisers, on the other hand, get roughly one shot per budget cycle to convince finance that a channel is working.

See also: Google Data Shows AI Search Users Moved Past Keywords, Your Content Hasn’t

Mohan calls this “an expansionary moment” for search. For most of Google’s history, she explains, people had to translate a curiosity into a keyword. That friction is fading, so “people are asking much longer, much more detailed questions.” More important for keyword lists, she says, “They’re asking net new types of queries that they would have never thought to ask before.”

Marvin asked the question many PPC managers would ask. Are people coming back with follow-ups because the first answer failed?

Mohan said no, and gave a detail I hadn’t heard Google explain before. “In our data science practice, we penalize queries, which we call man refs, manual refinements,” because a user having to add words means Google missed the original intent. By her account, the growth in follow-ups reflects new curiosity rather than bad answers.

For advertisers, that follow-up behavior is no longer just a user experience story. Google says it is launching multi-turn ads in AI Mode in the U.S., so ads can appear in follow-up queries, according to Shashi Thakur, VP and GM of Google Search Ads.

“What we’re seeing more and more of with AI is that people’s queries are kind of moving up the funnel,” Mohan said, with more brainstorming questions and more questions about how to think about a category altogether.

She also described what shoppers do when they get close to a decision. When people compare two products, “they really don’t want to just see the summary.” They want reviews, podcasts, and videos from people in their own circumstances.

See also: How AI Search Trends Are Changing PPC Campaign Structures – Ask A PPC

Mohan says query length has increased three times, and Marvin repeats it in her wrap-up as queries in conversational search “running three times longer on average.” Google’s published version is narrower. Its AI Mode Report (U.S., May 2026), as cited by Thakur, says queries in AI Mode are on average three times longer than traditional searches. That is an AI Mode comparison, not a claim about all Google searches.

Mohan also says, “one in every six searches is multimodal.” Google’s May report put it at more than one in six AI Mode searches in the U.S. And on the Q3 call in October, CEO Sundar Pichai gave a newer, global figure, saying “one in five AI Mode searches globally use voice, images or documents.”

To her credit, Mohan explained how Google vets the numbers it releases. Her team checks that an insight is repeatable, and “we don’t want to be able to put out any number that is at a peak point or is something that is liable to change.”

I take her at her word on that rigor. But the durable number advertisers need most is one Google hasn’t published, which is how ads in AI Mode actually perform. Alphabet’s 10-Q doesn’t break out paid clicks or cost-per-click for AI Mode. The only performance figure in Thakur’s post is that click-through rates are “already similar between sponsored and organic product list items within AI Mode,” based on Google internal data from Aug. 20 to Sept. 3, 2026. That’s two weeks of data, measured by Google, comparing ads to organic listings rather than to traditional Shopping ads. Treat it as Google’s claim until your own accounts confirm it.

See also: How To Leverage AI Ad Placements For Stronger PPC Performance

The podcast and the earnings release went out the same day, and the release is where the up-funnel story gets a price tag.

Alphabet reported Google Search & other revenue of $63.35 billion in Q3 2026, up 12% year-over-year, after growth of 19% in Q1 and 17% in Q2. Sequentially it was nearly flat, up from $63.27 billion in Q2. The 10-Q, filed Oct. 29, shows paid clicks on Google Search & other up 8% year-over-year, down from 13% in each of the first two quarters, while cost-per-click rose 4%.

Meanwhile, Pichai said query growth was the highest it has been in five years, including commercial queries.

When JPMorgan’s Doug Anmuth asked why queries were growing faster than Search revenue, Google’s SVP and Chief Business Officer Philipp Schindler pointed first to currency, which he called the largest driver of the slowdown versus Q2. Then he added, “The strong growth in AI Mode queries had an impact because of the lower monetization we were seeing in AI Mode.”

Schindler’s optimistic case lines up neatly with Mohan’s research. “On AI Mode, many of these queries are upper-funnel queries, allowing us to capture new high-value commercial intent,” he said, adding that “the majority of AI Mode queries are incremental, creating more monetization opportunities over time.” Google is now scaling ads in AI Overviews and AI Mode to more users and queries and expanding AI Mode ads to international markets.

Pichai offered his own historical frame on the call, comparing this moment to the shift to mobile, when Google focused first on the user experience and then on monetization. Anyone who managed paid search in that era remembers how it felt. In Q3 2016, Alphabet reported paid clicks up 33% and cost-per-click down 11%, according to Reuters, as searches moved to smaller screens with cheaper ads.

Here is where I think the analogy breaks. In the mobile transition, the monetization gap showed up as cheaper clicks. This time, cost-per-click is still rising, and the gap shows up as queries growing faster than paid clicks. Advertisers aren’t getting a discount for the move up the funnel. They’re getting new top-of-funnel inventory, priced by the same auction, and the burden of proving it pays off.

Marvin closed the episode with her own takeaways for advertisers, starting with a call to “recalibrate how they’re thinking about search” outside of last-click action. Here is how I’d turn that into work this week.

1. Confirm you’re eligible for the new inventory at all. Marvin reminded listeners that “ads in AI mode and AI overviews are matched to both the query and the content of the AI response.” Google’s help documentation for ads in AI Overviews says that only broad match keywords or keywordless targeting (AI Max for Search, Performance Max, Shopping, and Dynamic Search Ads) can serve ads inside the overview. Exact match can still serve above or below it, and Marvin has clarified that an exact match keyword won’t block the broad match version from triggering inside the overview. If your core non-brand campaigns run exact and phrase only, you’re opting out of the placements Google is scaling. Keep exact match where you need control, and give broad match or AI Max a defined slice of non-brand budget with Smart Bidding and clean conversion data behind it.

2. Fill the six conversational attributes in Merchant Center before Black Friday. Marvin flagged these optional fields, which Google rolled out at Google Marketing Live in May. They are question_and_answer, document_link, related_product, item_group_title, variant_option, and popularity_rank. They don’t affect product approval, and Google recommends adding them through a supplemental data source, so your primary feed stays untouched. Mohan’s point about comparison shoppers wanting real-world detail maps directly to question_and_answer and related_product. Start with your top sellers, since Black Friday falls on Nov. 27 this year.

3. Build a long-query segment in your reporting. Export your search terms report and group terms by word count, for example, one to three words, four to seven, and eight or more. Then compare conversion rate, cost per acquisition or ROAS, and how often each group appears in conversion paths. If the long queries convert poorly on last click but show up as earlier touches, you have the upper-funnel evidence finance will ask for. If they don’t show up anywhere, you’ve found where broad match or AI Max is spending on discovery you can’t yet justify, and you can tighten with negatives and brand controls. Either way, don’t let a blended CPA hide the difference.

4. Fix the page the ad sends people to. Marvin said, “Your ad should position your business as the natural next step in that user journey” and urged advertisers to “focus on reducing post-click friction.” A searcher who typed a 25-word question about a specific situation won’t convert on a generic category page. If you use AI Max’s final URL expansion, Google chooses the landing page from your site, so thin pages get picked for nuanced queries. Audit your highest-spend landing pages for the specifics a follow-up question would ask about, such as comparisons, sizing, pricing, and use cases. As Marvin noted, much of this sits outside the PPC team, so bring in your SEO, content, and CRO counterparts.

Mohan’s bicycle-design lesson applies to advertisers more than she may have intended. Google has the logs, the UXR interviews, and the ability to ship changes daily. Advertisers have the auction bill and a quarterly budget review.

My view is that Google is right about where search behavior is heading and wrong if it expects advertisers to fund that shift on faith. Until Google reports AI Mode ad performance with the same durability standard Mohan described for user statistics, I’d treat AI Mode and AI Overview reach as a test line with its own budget and its own success metric, rather than something that quietly drags down blended CPA.

Google checks its numbers for durability before it publishes them. Your CFO will expect you to do the same.

Fossil fuel emissions are down in 2026. Green energy isn’t the reason

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

The decrease is probably temporary and a result of high gas prices from the Iran war

The disruption of oil shipments through the Strait of Hormuz off the coast of Iran has raised oil and gas prices around the world. According to new projections, those higher fuel prices will decrease global carbon dioxide emissions from fossil fuel burning in 2026 by about 0.16 gigatons from 2025.

This is a human-written story voiced by AI. Got feedback? Take our survey . (See our AI policy here .)

Scientists project that global carbon dioxide emissions from fossil fuels will decrease by 0.4 percent this year, after having grown by 0.8 percent each year on average from 2019 to 2025.

But the dip may not last. The lower emissions are probably driven by high oil prices stemming from the Iran war, researchers report November 9 in Nature Climate Change.

Global emissions probably would have grown again this year without the energy shock of the Iran war, says Glen Peters, a climate scientist at the Center for International Climate Research in Oslo, Norway. “So the energy transition that’s happening … it’s not quite big enough to get emissions heading downward yet.”

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

For the study, Peters and his colleagues used data on countries’ electric vehicle stocks and wind and solar energy generation to project those technologies impacts on global fossil CO2 emissions through 2028. The team used the generative AI model Claude to source data and write code for analysis.

By year’s end, global emissions will reach 38.24 gigatons, the researchers found, which is about 0.16 gigatons less than were emitted in 2025. For comparison, annual global emissions fell by nearly two gigatons in 2020 due to the COVID-19 pandemic and by about 0.5 gigatons in 2009 because of the global financial crisis.

If electric vehicle sales and solar and wind capacity continue growing at the rates they did in 2026, those technologies should counteract about 63 percent of the growth in emissions from 2025 to 2028 — or help avoid about 1.64 gigatons of added emissions.

The results indicate that the energy transition needs to accelerate if humankind is to realize consistently declining emissions, though EVs, solar and wind are already having a large impact. Without the offset in emissions provided by those technologies, global emissions would have risen by 1.9 percent each year on average from 2019 to 2025, the researchers note.

It’s very hard to say exactly how long it will take for humanity to reach peak emissions, Peters says. However, he notes, “the fact that we’re talking about this means that we’re close to that point.”

C. Le Quéré et al. A further acceleration of clean energy is needed for global CO2 emissions to peak and decline. Nature Climate Change. 2026. doi: 10.1038/s41558-026-02821-8
